3.3.
Symplectic Jeu de Taquin

In this section we briefly recall the symplectic jeu de taquin procedure
[
She99
,
Lec02
,
San21a
]
.

Definition 3.14
.

Given a column
C\in\mathrm{KN}^{C}_{n}{(1^{k})}
with
\mathrm{split}(C)=lCrC
, let
\Phi(C)
be the tableau of shape
(1^{k})
obtained by combining the unbarred entries from
lC
and barred entries from
rC
.

In particular,
\Phi(C)=C
if and only if
C
does not have symmetric entries. The column
\Phi(C)
is a
co-admissible column
and the procedure to form
\Phi(C)
from
C
is reversible (for details see
[
Lec02
,
San21b
, Section 2.2]
or
[
AS24
, Section 2]
). In particular, every column on the alphabet
[n]
is simultaneously admissible and co-admissible. The map
\Phi
is a bijection between admissible and co-admissible columns of the same height.

Example 3.15
.

Consider the admissible column
C
of shape
(1^{3})
with reading word
w(C)=23\bar{2}
, so that
w(lC)=13\bar{2}
,
w(rC)=23\bar{1}
. Then,
\Phi(C)
is the column of shape
(1^{3})
with reading word
w(\Phi(C))=13\bar{1}
. Also consider the column
C^{\prime}
with reading word
w(C^{\prime})=24\bar{2}
which is simultaneously admissible and co-admissible. Then
\Phi(C^{\prime})
is the column of shape
(1^{3})
with reading word
w(\Phi(C^{\prime}))=14\bar{1}
and
w(\Phi^{-1}(C^{\prime}))=34\bar{3}
.

Let
T
be a punctured symplectic KN skew tableau
4
4

4

In the literature the punctured cell is often assigned with the symbol
*
. Here is just a blank cell as in JDT, and the symbol
*
in a box indicates a (potentially) filled box.
with two columns
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
with the puncture
in
C_{1}
and
\mathrm{split}(T)=lC_{1}rC_{1}lC_{2}rC_{2}
.
Let
\alpha
be the entry under the puncture of
rC_{1}
, and
\beta
the entry to the right of the puncture of
rC_{1}
, that is, locally, the tableaux
T=C_{1}C_{2}
and
\mathrm{split}(T)=lC_{1}rC_{1}lC_{2}rC_{2}
look like:

\hbox{$\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$b$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}\kern-0.4pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$a$\hss}\hrule}\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\ast$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}}$}\qquad\xrightarrow{\mathrm{split}}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\beta$\hss}\hrule}\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\ast$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}\kern-0.4pt\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\ast$\hss}\hrule}\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\alpha$\hss}\hrule}\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\ast$\hss}\hrule}\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\ast$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}}$}\;,

where either
a
or
b
may not exist. The elementary steps of the symplectic jeu de taquin, or SJDT for short, are the following:

A.

Suppose
a
exists. If
\alpha\leq\beta
or
\beta
does not exist, then the puncture in
T
will change its position with the cell beneath it. This is called a
vertical slide
.

\vtop{\halign{&\cellify{#}\cr\vrule width=0.0pt,height=10.0pt,depth=0.0pt\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss\\\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\ast$\hss}\vss\crcr}}\qquad\mapsto\qquad\vtop{\halign{&\cellify{#}\cr\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss\\\vrule width=0.0pt,height=10.0pt,depth=0.0pt\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\ast$\hss}\vss\crcr}}}}}}}}}}

B.

Suppose
b
exists. If
\alpha>\beta
or
\alpha
does not exist we say the slide is
horizontal
.
Let
C_{1}^{\prime}
and
C_{2}^{\prime}
be the columns of
T
obtained after the slide.

\vtop{\halign{&\cellify{#}\cr\vrule width=0.0pt,height=10.0pt,depth=0.0pt\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss\\\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\ast$\hss}\vss\crcr}}\qquad\mapsto\qquad\vtop{\halign{&\cellify{#}\cr\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$b^{\prime}$\hss}\vss&\vrule width=0.0pt,height=10.0pt,depth=0.0pt\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss\\\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$a^{\prime}$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\ast$\hss}\vss\crcr}}}}}}}}}}

There are two cases depending on the sign of
\beta
:

(1)

If
\beta
is barred then
b=\beta
and
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are obtained by setting
C_{1}^{\prime}:=\Phi^{-1}\left(\Phi(C_{1})\sqcup\hbox{$\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\beta$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}}$}\right)
and
C_{2}^{\prime}:=C_{2}\setminus\hbox{$\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\beta$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}}$}
.

(2)

If
\beta
is unbarred, then set
C_{1}^{\prime}:=C_{1}\sqcup\hbox{$\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\beta$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}}$}
with
a^{\prime}=a
,
b^{\prime}=\beta
, and
C_{2}^{\prime}:=\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(C_{2})\setminus\hbox{$\vbox{\moveright 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hrule\kern-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\vrule\hss$\beta$\hss}\hrule}\vrule}}$})
. This case, however, may yield a column
C_{1}^{\prime}
that is no longer a type
C
KN tableau. When this occurs we modify
C_{1}^{\prime}
as follows:

- Let
i
be the smallest value such that
C_{1}^{\prime}
contains the pair
(i,\bar{i})
with
N(i)>i
.

- Construct
C_{1}^{\prime\prime}
from
C_{1}^{\prime}
by removing the topmost and bottommost cells from
C_{1}^{\prime}
and labeling the boxes linearly with the entries from
C_{1}^{\prime}
, skipping over
i
and
\bar{i}
.

- Replace
C_{1}^{\prime}
with
C_{1}^{\prime\prime}
.

Iterative application of this procedure will eventually yield a left justified shape, that is for every puncture both
a
and
b
will not exists. When this occurs,
symplectic jeu de taquin
ends.

Remark 3.16
.

It is important to note that SJDT is reversible by pushing the filled cells to the right and the empty cells to the left. The reverse process is denoted RSJDT.

The
rectification

\operatorname{rect}_{C}(T)
of a a symplectic KN skew tableau
T
is the tableau of partition shape obtained by iterated applications of SJDT. In particular, rectification is independent of the order in which the inner corners are filled
[
Lec02
, Corollary 6.3.9]
.

Remark 3.17
.

If the columns
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
do not have barred entries then the SJDT coincides with the
jeu de taquin
on semistandard Young tableaux.

Remark 3.18
.

The rectification map on words, defined by the
B_{n}
-spin insertion or
B_{n}
(
C_{n}
)-insertion scheme,
w\otimes w^{\prime}\mapsto[\emptyset\leftarrow w\leftarrow w^{\prime}]
is a
B_{n}
(
C_{n}
)-crystal isomorphism
[
Bak00a
,
Lec03
]
. In type
C_{n}
it coincides with the rectification defined by SJDT on the diagonal skew tableau with reading word
w\otimes w^{\prime}
.

Example 3.19
.

The following computation shows an example of rectification using SJDT.

3.4.
Virtualization and Jeu de Taquin in Type B

We build on the virtualization procedure described in
[
PPSS24
]
where virtual crystals are provided for spin and vector (or standard) representation
of type
B_{n}
into type
C_{n}
. They are crystal isomorphic to those obtained in the tableau crystal by the
splitting
map, defined above, viewed as a virtualization map from tableau crystals of type
B_{n}
to ones of type
C_{n}
. Recall that for a chosen Dynkin embedding the virtualization map is unique and by
Proposition
1.9
virtualization is closed for the tensor product.

In this section we will use the Dynkin diagram embeddings
\psi_{BC}:B_{n}\to C_{n}
and
\psi_{CB}:C_{n}\to B_{n}

considered in
[
Fuj18
,
PPSS24
]
(see Table
1
). It follows from their definition that the action of both these virtualization coincides on the spin and standard representation. Since virtualization commutes with the tensor product (see Proposition
1.9
), then the virtualizations in
[
Fuj18
,
PPSS24
]
are the same. Thus, we will use both definitions intercheangably and denote them
\text{\Aries}_{BC}
and
\text{\Aries}_{CB}
.

We will also make ample usage of the
symplectic insertion

[
Bak00a
,
Lec02
,
KS04
]
and
orthogonal insertion

[
Lec03
,
KS04
]
on skew tableaux, referring the readers to these sources for details.

The following proposition can also be found in
[
Fuj18
, Sec. 6]
.

Proposition 3.20
.

The composition of the virtualization maps
\text{\Aries}_{BC}
and
\text{\Aries}_{CB}
yield
2
-dilations. Namely,

\text{\Aries}_{CB}\circ\text{\Aries}_{BC}=\Theta_{2}^{B}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{\Aries}_{BC}\circ\text{\Aries}_{CB}=\Theta_{2}^{C}.

Proof.

From Theorem
2.5
we know that any
m
-dilation is a virtualization. Since the compositions of virtualizations is again a virtualization and by Remark
1.8
, virtualizations map highest weight vectors to highest weight vectors, it suffices to show that the images of the crystal operators under both maps coincide.

So suppose
\text{\Aries}_{BC}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{V}\subset\tilde{B}
and
\text{\Aries}_{CB}:\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\to\tilde{\mathcal{V}}
. Then for any
b\in\mathcal{B}
with
f_{i}(b)\neq 0
if
i\neq n
, we have that:

\text{\Aries}_{CB}(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(f_{i}(b)))=\text{\Aries}_{CB}(f_{i}^{2}(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(b)))=f_{i}^{2}(\text{\Aries}_{CB}(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(b))).

If instead
i=n
, then:

\text{\Aries}_{CB}(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(f_{n}(b)))=\text{\Aries}_{CB}(f_{n}(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(b)))=f_{n}^{2}(\text{\Aries}_{CB}(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(b))).

Thus,
\text{\Aries}_{CB}\circ\text{\Aries}_{BC}
coincides with the
2
-dilation map
\Theta_{2}
in type
B_{2}
. The reverse composition follows similarly.
∎

It is important to note that although the images of
\Theta_{2}(\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
and
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
coincide as graphs, they are not equivalent as crystal embeddings. Indeed,
\Theta_{2}
yields an embedding from type
B_{n}
onto itself, unlike
\text{\Aries}_{BC}
which embeds
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
onto a type
C_{n}
crystal.

Example 3.21
.

Let
n=1
. Consider the
B_{1}

We will now show that the virtualization map
\text{\Aries}_{BC}
on KN tableaux is nothing more than the splitting map.

Theorem 3.22
.

Suppose
T\in\mathrm{KN}^{B}_{n}{(\lambda)}
with
\lambda=(1^{n}|\emptyset)
or
\lambda=(\emptyset|1^{k})
with
1\leq k<n
. Then,
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=\mathrm{split}(T)
.

Proof.

We begin by noting that
\Psi_{BC}(i)=i
for any vertex
i\in B_{n}
. Thus, the virtualization maps
\omega_{i}^{B}\mapsto 2\omega_{i}^{C}
for
i\neq n
and
\omega_{n}^{B}\mapsto\omega_{n}^{C}
. Consequently, for any
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{a_{i}\omega^{B}_{i}}
since
\omega_{i}^{C}=\omega_{i}^{B}
for
i\neq n
and
\omega_{n}^{C}=2\omega_{n}^{B}
, it follows that
\mu\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{2a_{i}\omega^{C}_{i}}+a_{n}\omega_{n}^{C}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{2a_{i}\omega^{B}_{i}}+a_{n}2\omega_{n}^{B}=2\mu
.

So consider the spin case when
\lambda=(1^{n}|\emptyset)=\omega_{n}^{B}
. Then, for any
T\in\mathrm{KN}^{B}_{n}{(1^{n}|\emptyset)}
,
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)
is a tableau in
\mathrm{KN}^{C}_{n}{(1^{n})}
with weight
2\mathrm{wt}(T)
. Since by definition, a spin KN tableau has no pairs
(z,\bar{z})
for any value of
z
, it follows that every
T\in\mathrm{KN}^{B}_{n}{(1^{n}|\emptyset)}
, and thus its image in
\mathrm{KN}^{C}_{n}{(1^{n})}
, is uniquely determined by its weight. Since
\mathrm{wt}(\mathrm{split}(T))=2\mathrm{wt}(T)
it follows that
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=\mathrm{split}(T)
.

Consider now the non-spin case
\lambda=(\emptyset|1^{k})
for some
k\neq n
. Given any
T\in\mathrm{KN}^{B}_{n}{(\emptyset|1^{k})}
, let
T^{\prime}
be the KN tableau of skew shape
(k,k-1,\dots,1)/(k-1,k-2,\dots,1,0)

with
w(T^{\prime})=w(T)=x_{1}\dots x_{m}
(see Example
3.23
).

Now, recall that the virtualization
\text{\Aries}_{BC}
, as defined in
[
PPSS24
]
, is defined on the standard representation
\mathcal{B}(\omega_{1})

via the map

i\mapsto ii,\bar{i}\mapsto\bar{i}\bar{i},0\mapsto n\bar{n}\hbox{ for all }1\leq i\leq n

and induced on words via the tensor product on crystals by setting

\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T))=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(x_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{m})):=w(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{m})),

so that
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T))]
.
In particular, since
w(T)=w(T^{\prime})
, then
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T^{\prime})
. Evidently, since
\mathrm{split}(T^{\prime})
is simply a duplication of the columns of
T^{\prime}
, then

w(\mathrm{split}(T^{\prime}))=w(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{m}))=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T^{\prime}))

Moreover, by
[
Lec02
, Corollary 6.3.9]
we know that for any type C KN tableau rectification via SJDT coincides with type C insertion of its reading word. Thus, it follows that:

(30)

\operatorname{rect}_{C}(\mathrm{split}(T^{\prime}))=[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}w(\mathrm{split}(T^{\prime}))]=[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T^{\prime}))]=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T).

On the other hand, by
[
Lec03
, Proposition 3.5.3]

also have that

\operatorname{rect}_{C}(\mathrm{split}(T^{\prime}))=\mathrm{split}[\emptyset\xleftarrow{B}w(T^{\prime})]=\mathrm{split}[\emptyset\xleftarrow{B}w(T)]=\mathrm{split}(T),

so that
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=\mathrm{split}(T)
as desired.
∎

Example 3.23
.

Let
n=3
and
consider the type
B_{3}
column tableau
T=\vtop{\halign{&\cellify{#}\cr\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$0$\hss}\vss\\\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$0$\hss}\vss\\\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\bar{1}$\hss}\vss\crcr}}}}}
with
\mathrm{split}(T)=\vtop{\halign{&\cellify{#}\cr\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\bar{3}$\hss}\vss\\\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$3$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\bar{2}$\hss}\vss\\\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\bar{1}$\hss}\vss&\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(13.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,13.0){\line{1}{0}{13.0}}\end{picture}}\hss}\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\bar{1}$\hss}\vss\crcr}}}}}}}}
. Then we have,

T^{\prime}=\vtop{\halign{&\cellify{#}\cr\vrule width=0.0pt,height=10.0pt,depth=0.0pt\vbox to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss&\vrule width=0.0pt,height=10.0pt,depth=0.0pt\vbox Computing, we can see that
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}\text{\Aries}_{BC}(00\bar{1})]=[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}3\bar{3}3\bar{3}\bar{1}\bar{1}]
. From Remark
3.17
, we know that
[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}3\bar{3}3\bar{3}\bar{1}\bar{1}]
equals to the SJDT rectification of
\mathrm{split}(T^{\prime})
. We then obtain by SJDT:

to13.0pt{\vss\hbox to13.0pt{\hss$\bar{1}$\hss}\vss\crcr}}=\mathrm{split}(T)}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}

so that, indeed,
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=\operatorname{rect}_{C}(\mathrm{split}(T^{\prime}))
.

Corollary 3.24
.

For any
T\in\mathrm{KN}^{B}_{n}{(\mu_{0}|\mu)}
we have that
\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T)=\mathrm{split}(T)
.

Proof.

Recall that by definition
\mathrm{split}(T)=\mathrm{split}(T_{0})\mathrm{split}(T_{1})\cdots\mathrm{split}(T_{\mu_{1}})
where
T_{i}
is the
i^{th}
column of
T
, with
T_{0}
the (potentially empty) spin column. Since by Theorem
3.22

\mathrm{split}(T)=\mathrm{split}(T_{0})\mathrm{split}(T_{1})\cdots\mathrm{split}(T_{\mu_{1}})=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{0})\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{1})\cdots\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{\mu_{1}})

it follows that,

\displaystyle w(\mathrm{split}(T))

\displaystyle=w(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{0})\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{1})\cdots\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{\mu_{1}}))=w(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{\mu_{1}}))\cdots w(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{1}))w(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T_{0}))

\displaystyle=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T_{\mu_{1}}))\cdots\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T_{1}))\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T_{0}))=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T_{\mu_{1}})\cdots w(T_{1})w(T_{0}))

\displaystyle=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T))

Thus, the result follows by performing type C insertion:

\mathrm{split}(T)=[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}w(\mathrm{split}(T))]=[\emptyset\xleftarrow{C}\text{\Aries}_{BC}(w(T))]=\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T).

∎

The virtual crystal

.

A nice consequence of the discussion above is that the
orthogonal jeu de taquin
(BJDT), defined in
[
Lec03
]
, can be reformulated in terms of the virtualization
\text{\Aries}_{BC}
and SJDT as the pullback of SJDT by
\text{\Aries}_{BC}^{-1}=(\mathrm{split})^{-1}
.

Corollary 3.25
.

Let
T
be any orthogonal skew KN tableau with a potentially nonempty spin column. Then,

\text{\Aries}_{BC}\circ\operatorname{rect}_{B}(T)=\operatorname{rect}_{C}\circ\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T).

Proof.

This is immediate from the arguments above where virtualization is first performed on words and then inserted. Namely, if
w(T)=x_{1}\cdots x_{m}
denotes the reading word of
T
, then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{m}\in\mathcal{B}(\omega_{1}^{B})^{\otimes m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{m})\in\mathcal{B}(2\omega^{C}_{1})^{\otimes m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T=[\emptyset\leftarrow x_{1}\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow x_{m}]\in\mathrm{KN}^{B}_{n}{(\lambda)}{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{split}(T)=[\emptyset\leftarrow\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{1})\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\text{\Aries}_{BC}(x_{m})]\in\mathrm{KN}^{C}_{n}{(2\lambda)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{\Aries}_{BC})^{\otimes m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}-insertion}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{n}-insertion}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\Aries}_{BC}}

∎

3.5.
Applications to Keys and Orthogonal Evacuation

In what follows we give a couple of combinatorial consequences of the work in Sections
1
and
2
. The first result is a combinatorial characterization of orthogonal keys.

Proposition 3.26
.

For any
T\in\mathrm{KN}^{B}_{n}{(\lambda)}
, we have that
T
is an extremal weight vector if and only if the columns of
T
are nested with no pairs
(i,\bar{i})
appearing in any column for any
0\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.18
,
T
is a type
B_{n}
left (resp. right) key if and only if
\mathrm{split}(T)
is a type
C_{n}
left (resp. right) key. A symplectic key is a
C_{n}
KN tableau where columns are nested and the letters
i
and
\bar{i}
, for any
1\leq i\leq n
, do not appear simultaneously as entries in a column
[
San21b
]
. Hence
T
is a key in type
B_{n}
if and only if the columns of
\mathrm{split}(T)
are nested. That is, the columns of
T
are nested and
no column contains pairs of the form
(i,\bar{i})
for any
i\neq 0
and no entry equal to zero. Since zero pairs with itself, the result follows.
∎

.

Consider the tableau

in Figure
1
. Then indeed, no column contains any pairs with
\{\bar{2}\}\subset\{1,\bar{2}\}
. A similar study of the other extremal weight vectors in
\mathcal{B}(1^{2}|1)
yields similar conclusions.

Now, recall that by Proposition
1.11
we know that virtualization commutes with the Lusztig–Schützenberger involution
\xi
. That is, if
♈
is a virtualization from
\mathcal{B}
to
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
then
\xi_{\mathcal{B}}=\text{\Aries}^{-1}\xi_{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}}\text{\Aries}
. Computing the Lusztig–Schützenberger involution on a given tableau coincides with so called
evacuation
.

For type
C
, Santos
[
San21b
,
San21a
]
developed a combinatorial method that directly computes this result without having to perform the involution on the entire crystal. In particular, given a symplectic tableau
T
, its evacuation
\operatorname{evac}_{C}(T)
is given by first barring the unbarred entries and vice versa, followed by performing a 180 degree rotation of the tableau, and then rectifying the diagram via SJDT.

For type
B_{n}
such a direct combinatorial method is not known. Nonetheless, since evacuation of a tableau coincides exactly with computing its image under the Lusztig–Schützenberger involution, then setting
\text{\Aries}=\text{\Aries}_{BC}=\mathrm{split}
and
\xi_{C}=\operatorname{evac}_{C}
(see
[
San21b
]
) we can compute orthogonal evacuation combinatorially by setting:

(31)

\operatorname{evac}_{B}:=(\mathrm{split})^{-1}\operatorname{evac}_{C}\mathrm{split}

It is natural to inquire what other types such a method can be applied. Theoretically, of course, the results are type independent. In practice, they are somewhat limited by the combinatorial tools available.
In particular, a jeu de taquin for type
D_{n}
is not known. The pullback of SJDT, via the virtualization
\text{\Aries}^{BC}
, utilized to define a jeu de taquin in type
B_{n}
does not work to define a jeu de taquin in type
D_{n}
as shown in
[
Lec03
]
.
Nonetheless, despite the fact that a type
D_{n}
jeu de taquin is not available one has an
D_{n}
-insertion scheme for crystals
[
KS04
,
Lec01
,
Lec07
]
. The evacuation in type
D_{n}
should proceed in a similar way by applying the longest element of the Weyl group of type
D_{n}
to the entries of the tableau and replacing the SJDT by the appropriate insertion in type
D_{n}
.

4.
Additional Examples

In this section we provide some explicit examples of how Theorems
2.8
,
2.14
, and
2.18
can be used to compute the left/right keys of a given orthogonal tableau.

4.1.
Applying Theorem
2.8

Consider the
B_{2}
-crystal
B(1^{2}|1)
in Figure
1
and its
3
-dilation
\Theta_{3}(B(1^{2}|1))
in Figure
3
. Then, by Theorem
2.8
we can compute the left and right key of any
b\in\mathcal{B}(1^{2}|1)
by looking at its image in
\Theta_{3}(B(1^{2}|1))
.

So let
T=\vtop{\halign{&\scellify{#}\cr\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{\begin{picture}\put(-0.2,-0.2){\hbox to13.2pt{\vbox to13.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}

On the other hand, since by Theorem
2.18

K^{+}(T)=\text{\Aries}_{BC}^{-1}\circ K^{+}(\text{\Aries}_{BC}(T))
then,
K^{+}(T)
can also be computed directly applying the procedure in
[
San21b
,
San21a
, Example 16]
and
[
AS24
, Example 5.2]
that uses symplectic jeu de taquin (SJDT) and reverse symplectic jeu de taquin (RSJDT) on
\mathrm{split}(T)
and then virtualizing back. Namely, applying RSJDT to

4.4.
Example of Theorem
2.8

Finally, we offer an example of the differences between the
3
and
6
-dilations of the
B_{2}
-crystal
\mathcal{B}(1^{2}|1)
seen in Figures
3
and
4
. Namely, by Theorem
2.8
, since the longest
i
-string in
\mathcal{B}(1^{2}|1)
has length three, it suffices to compute the
3
-dilations in order to find the left/right keys of any given tableau
T\in\mathcal{B}(1^{2}|1)
. On the other hand, observe that in general the intermediate tensor factor in
\mathcal{B}(1^{2}|1)
will not be an extremal weight vector. Take for instance,

is not.

, we see that all vectors in this tensor product are indeed extremal. It is easy to see that this property holds for all vectors in
\mathcal{B}(1^{2}|1)
. Indeed, although we know of no references for the following, we suspect this property is true in general. Namely, that
\Theta_{m}(b)\in\mathcal{O}(\lambda)^{\otimes m}
all
b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
if and only if
\ell
divides
m
where
\ell
is the is the least common multiple of the lengths of all the
i
-strings that originate at some extremal weight vector in
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
.
